# Deportation in the second Trump administration

For broader coverage of this topic, see [Immigration policy of the second Trump administration](78945778.md) and [Deportation from the United States](45009959.md).

For details on the deportation of American citizens, see [Deportations of U.S. citizens in the second Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportations_of_U.S._citizens_in_the_second_Trump_administration).

During [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s [second term](78298108.md) as [president of the United States](24113.md), his administration has pursued a [deportation policy](45009959.md) generally described as "[hardline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardline)",[^cite_note-Hardline-51] "maximalist",[^cite_note-52] and as a "mass deportation" campaign,[^cite_note-Chung_06022025-48] involving the detention, confinement, and expulsion of hundreds of thousands of immigrants and their family members.[^cite_note-Chung_06022025-48] The Trump administration has claimed that around 140,000 people had been deported as of April 2025, though some estimates put the number at roughly half that.[^cite_note-Villagran-53] On August 28, 2025, [CNN](62028.md) reported that [U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement](216537.md) (ICE) alone had deported nearly 200,000 people in seven months since Trump returned to office.[^cite_note-auto2-54] By January 2026, the number of people which ICE alone deported rose to roughly 540,000, which is down from the number of people deported under [Joe Biden](145422.md) in 2023 and 2024.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]&#x20;

The use of deportation flights by the U.S. has created pushback from some foreign governments, particularly [that of Colombia](76741.md).[^cite_note-usatoday_26-01-25-57] The total population of illegal immigrants in the United States was estimated at 11 million in 2022, with the top three states of [California](5407.md), [Texas](29810.md), and [Florida](18933066.md) constituting over half of the total population.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59]&#x20;

The administration has used the [Alien Enemies Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_Enemies_Act) to quickly deport immigrants with limited or no [due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process),[^cite_note-Ward_04282025-60][^cite_note-61] and to be [imprisoned in El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2025_American_deportations_of_Venezuelans), which was halted by federal judges and the [Supreme Court](31737.md).[^cite_note-ManganCNBC4.10-62][^cite_note-AP_04192025-63] Several US citizens have been [detained and deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportations_of_U.S._citizens_in_the_second_Trump_administration).[^cite_note-Danner_05032025-64] Administration practices have faced legal issues and controversy with lawyers, judges, and legal scholars.[^cite_note-Ward_04282025-60]&#x20;

At the time of the January 2025 start to Trump's second presidential term, a majority of Americans supported deporting all illegal immigrants present in the United States.[^cite_note-65] As early as April 2025, multiple polls found that the majority of Americans thought that the deportations went "too far".[^cite_note-Edwards-Levy_04302025-66][^cite_note-Linley_04252025-67][^cite_note-Marist_07012025-68][^cite_note-Sanders_07112025-69] In early 2026, [national protests against deportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_U.S._immigration_enforcement_protests) escalated after the killings of [Renée Good](82055905.md) and [Alex Pretti](82203651.md), both American citizens, by immigration agents. In July 2026, multiple vigils and protests broke out after an ICE agent shot and killed [Lorenzo Salgado Araujo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorenzo_Salgado_Araujo) in [Houston](13774.md),[^cite_note-70] followed several days later by an ICE agent fatally shooting [Johan Sebastián Durán Guerrero](83686932.md) in [Biddeford, Maine](116267.md).[^cite_note-71]&#x20;

Gregory Bovino

[Gregory Bovino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Bovino)

See also: [Noem v. Vasquez Perdomo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noem_v._Vasquez_Perdomo)

In January 2025, under the Biden administration, [U.S. Customs and Border Patrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Customs_and_Border_Patrol) conducted "Operation Return to Sender" in [Kern County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kern_County). Under the command of senior official [Gregory Bovino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Bovino), the operation has been described "as a blueprint for the broader immigration crackdown that was to come".[^cite_note-72] According to the case, United Farm Workers v. Noem filed by the [ACLU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACLU), Border Patrol agents were alleged to have gone "on a fishing expedition, dispensing with [reasonable suspicion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reasonable_suspicion) and stopping people based on assumptions about their race or occupation instead", "made [warrantless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrantless_searches_in_the_United_States) arrests", people arrested "were expelled to Mexico after being coerced to accept [voluntary departure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voluntary_departure)", "agents did not identify themselves", and "agents denied (...) repeated requests to call a lawyer".[^cite_note-73] In April 2025, Judge [Jennifer L. Thurston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jennifer_L._Thurston) granted a [preliminary injunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preliminary_injunction) forbidding Border Patrol from conducting warrantless stops and acting without reasonable suspicion in the [Eastern District of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_District_of_California).[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75]&#x20;

### 2024 campaign

Further information: [Donald Trump 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign)

Trump's campaign heavily relied on [fearmongering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fearmongering) against immigrants.[^cite_note-\:113-76][^cite_note-\:118-77][^cite_note-Gold_1012024-78] He repeatedly used [racial stereotypes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_stereotype) and [dehumanizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehumanization) rhetoric to paint the influx of illegal immigrants as an assault—an "invasion"[^cite_note-79]—on the American public,[^cite_note-Gold-Huynh_April_2_24-80][^cite_note-\:121-81] citing baseless accounts of their proclivity for crime.[^cite_note-\:114-82][^cite_note-\:115-83][^cite_note-\:116-84] On the campaign trail in December 2023, Trump said immigrants coming to the U.S. are "poisoning the blood of our country", a remark that quickly drew a rebuke from scholars, historians, and his political opponents likening the phrase to [racial hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_hygiene) rhetoric and language used by [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md).[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-Politico_Otterbein-87]&#x20;

*[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported that Trump planned "an extreme expansion of his first-term crackdown on immigration", including "preparing to round up undocumented people already in the United States on a vast scale and detain them in sprawling camps while they wait to be expelled", and that it "amounts to an assault on immigration on a scale unseen in modern American history". *The New York Times* also reported that Trump's advisors were preparing a 'blitz' strategy designed to overwhelm immigrant-rights lawyers, and that his plans would rely on existing statutes without the need for new legislation, although such legislation would also likely be attempted.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88] At the time, economic reports from the [Brookings Institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookings_Institution) and [Peterson Institute for International Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peterson_Institute_for_International_Economics) found that Trump's plans would result in a decrease in employment for American-born workers and result in "no economic growth over the second Trump administration from this policy alone"[^cite_note-89] while other estimates had it shrinking the American [gross domestic product](12594.md) by about 4-7%.[^cite_note-90]&#x20;

#### Policy intentions and proposals

During his rallies, Trump said he would revoke the [temporary protected status](20497716.md) of Haitian migrants living legally in the US and deport them back to Haiti.[^cite_note-Phifer_10032024-91] Trump did not rule out separating families with mixed citizenship status.[^cite_note-92]&#x20;

The [American Immigration Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Immigration_Council) said that a "highly conservative" estimate of Trump's plan would come in at least \$315 billion, or \$967.9 billion over a decade, and be unworkable without massive outdoor detention camps.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88]&#x20;

Trump stated that his plan would follow the 'Eisenhower model,' a reference to the 1954 campaign [Operation Wetback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Wetback). To achieve the goal of deporting millions per year, Trump stated his intent to expand [expedited removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedited_removal) pursuant to section 235 of the [Immigration and Nationality Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1952)*;* invoke the [Alien Enemies Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_and_Sedition_Acts#Alien_Enemies_Act) within the [Alien and Sedition Acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_and_Sedition_Acts) of 1798; and invoke the [Insurrection Act of 1807](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurrection_Act_of_1807) to allow the military to apprehend immigrants and bypass the [Posse Comitatus Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posse_Comitatus_Act).[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88]&#x20;

Trump told a rally audience in September 2024 that the deportation effort "will be a bloody story".[^cite_note-Graziosi-93] He also spoke of rounding up homeless people in Democratic-leaning cities and detaining them in camps.[^cite_note-Brownstein-94] The Trump team said they would also attempt to overturn the [Flores settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reno_v._Flores) that prevents the indefinite holding of children.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88]&#x20;



Trump pledged to finish [the wall on the southern border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall) during his 2024 presidential campaign.

Trump promised to reinstate [his ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_travel_ban) on entry to individuals from certain Muslim-majority nations, and have the [Centers for Disease Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Disease_Control) reimpose COVID-era [restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_42_expulsions) on asylum claims by asserting immigrants carry infectious diseases such as the flu, tuberculosis, and scabies.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88] Trump said he would build more of the [border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall), and move thousands of troops currently stationed overseas to the southern border.[^cite_note-AP_Trump_Plans-95]&#x20;

Other proposals included revoking [temporary protected status](20497716.md) for individuals living in the United States, ending [birthright citizenship](7545952.md) for persons born in the United States to foreign parents, using coercive diplomacy by making immigration cooperation a condition for bilateral engagement, reinstating '[Remain in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remain_in_Mexico)' and reviving '[safe third country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_third_country)' status with several nations in Central America, as well as expanding it to Africa, Asia, and South America.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88][^cite_note-Reuters_Trump_Plans-96]&#x20;

Trump's campaign stated his intention to expel [Deferred Action for Childhood Arrivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deferred_Action_for_Childhood_Arrivals) recipients after his previous attempt failed in 2020 by a 5–4 vote in the Supreme Court in *[Department of Homeland Security v. Regents of the University of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Homeland_Security_v._Regents_of_the_University_of_California)*.[^cite_note-NYT_Immigration-88]&#x20;

## Implementation actions



[Tom Homan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Homan), Trump's [border czar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_czar)

### Policy changes

On January 22, 2025, the [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) (DHS) announced the administration was rolling back an Obama-era directive that had protected undocumented immigrants in sensitive areas such as hospitals, places of worship, courtrooms, funerals, weddings and schools. A spokesperson stated that the Trump administration was not looking to tie the hands of law enforcement.[^cite_note-newrepublic_22-01-25-97][^cite_note-ap_21-01-25-98]&#x20;

On January 23, Acting DHS Secretary [Benjamine Huffman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamine_Huffman) sent out a memo stating that immigrants admitted temporarily by the Biden administration could be removed.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

On February 19, the Trump administration issued a stop-work order to prevent the [Office of Refugee Resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Refugee_Resettlement) from funding organizations providing legal services to unaccompanied minors entering the United States; *[Mother Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Jones_\(magazine\))* said the order stopped legal representation for 25,000 minors and education programs on rights for 100,000 others.[^cite_note-100]&#x20;

On March 14, [United States Attorney General](44002.md) [Pam Bondi](4550623.md) issued a directive allowing law enforcement officials to enter the homes of migrants [without warrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrantless_searches_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102]&#x20;

As deportation flights have increased, airlines have increasingly sought to hide details about their planes from flight trackers.[^cite_note-103]&#x20;

On July 4, President Trump signed into law the [One Big Beautiful Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Big_Beautiful_Bill_Act), which included \$170 billion in spending for border security, the biggest increase in funding to immigration enforcement in US history.[^cite_note-104] The bill, which stated a goal of deporting one million immigrants yearly,[^cite_note-105] also made ICE "the most heavily funded law enforcement agency in the federal government."[^cite_note-106]&#x20;

In November, federal officials announced plans to hire thousands of "skip tracers" to conduct online and in-person verification of the home and work addresses of up to 1.5 million people living in the US.[^cite_note-\:10-107]&#x20;

On January 7, 2026, the same day that an ICE agent shot and killed [Renée Good](82055905.md) in Minnesota, Vice President [JD Vance](53396477.md) promised an escalation in immigration enforcement actions and policies. He said that over 10,000 additional ICE agents would be deployed in the United States, using personal data harvested by private contractors to identify targets for arrest. He said that agents would go "door to door" in the coming months to carry out Trump's mass deportation agenda.[^cite_note-\:10-107]&#x20;

#### Diversion and support from other government agencies

On January 23, 2025, the DHS authorized federal law enforcement to assist in carrying out Trump immigration policies. A memo from DHS official Benjamine Huffman granted "the functions of an immigration officer" to several agencies within the [U.S. Department of Justice](52563.md) including the [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md), the [Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Alcohol,_Tobacco,_Firearms_and_Explosives), and the [U.S. Marshals Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Marshals_Service).[^cite_note-108] In April 2025, the [Postal Inspection Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postal_Inspection_Service) began cooperating with federal immigration enforcement efforts.[^cite_note-109]&#x20;

In July, the [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md) increased the number of authorized military personnel designated to support ICE from 1,200 to 1,700 and rotated in National Guard personnel while sending Marines and Naval Reserve personnel to their home stations.[^cite_note-110]&#x20;

In August, Trump froze training for non-ICE federal agencies through the end of the year to accommodate immigration enforcement hires.[^cite_note-111]&#x20;

In September 2025, the [Cato Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cato_Institute) found that ICE had diverted over 25,000 federal, state and local law enforcement officers from their jobs. Of those 25,000, 14,498 were from federal agencies and 8,501 were state and local police.[^cite_note-112] FBI data from October 2025 found that nearly 1/4 of FBI agents, including nearly half in major offices, had been reassigned to support immigration enforcement.[^cite_note-113]&#x20;

By November 2025, immigration cases had overtaken drug and fraud cases in US courts. The number of immigration cases tripled on an annual basis while new fraud and drug cases fell by 17% and 27% respectively.[^cite_note-114]&#x20;

### Revocation of legal status

In May 2025, the Supreme Court permitted the Trump administration to revoke the protected status of 350,000 Venezuelan immigrants.[^cite_note-115] This action was described as "the largest single action stripping any group of non-citizens of immigration status in modern U.S. history".[^cite_note-116]&#x20;

## Raids and detention

This section is an excerpt from [Immigration detention in the second Trump administration](82451189.md).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration\&action=edit)]

|  | It has been suggested that parts of [Terrorism Confinement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center) ([Detention of foreign prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center#Detention_of_foreign_prisoners)) be [moved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Section_move) into this page. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Terrorism_Confinement_Center#Detention_of_foreign_prisoners_section_move_proposal)) *(February 2026)* |
| --- | --- |



Average daily population of detained immigrants held by the United States government for the fiscal years 1994-2025



The number of people arrested by ICE increased in the second term of Donald Trump, expanding to include greater numbers who had no criminal record.

[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_WashPost_20260828-117]

[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_NYTimes_20260827-118]

The [second Trump administration](78298108.md) has expanded [immigration detention in the United States](27272402.md) as part of the administration's mass deportation policy. On January 23, 2025, ICE [began to carry out raids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_immigration_raids_and_arrests_in_the_second_Trump_presidency) on [sanctuary cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city), with hundreds of [immigrants detained](1700791.md) and [deported](412990.md). The Trump administration reversed the policy of the previous administration and gave ICE permission to raid schools, hospitals and [places of worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Places_of_worship).[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_newrepublic_22-01-25-119][^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_ap_21-01-25-120] Fears of ICE raids have negatively impacted the [agriculture](242778.md),[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_newrepublic_22-01-25-119] construction,[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_npr_23-11-24-121] tourism,[^cite_note-122] and hospitality industries.[^cite_note-123] ICE agents conducting raids frequently travel in unmarked vehicles, wear plainclothes and facial coverings, and refuse to identify themselves or present warrants.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] The administration ordered the re-opening of the [Guantanamo Bay detention camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp) to hold potentially tens of thousands of illegal aliens,[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_nyt_29-01-25-128][^cite_note-129] but has faced logistical and legal difficulties using it as an immigrant camp.[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_\:4-130] The majority of detentions have been for non-violent matters.[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_Hagopian_06232025-131][^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_Tolan_06162025-132][^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_Bier_06202025-133] Courts have ruled more than 4,400 times that the Trump administration is detaining people illegally.[^cite_note-134] Several US citizens have been [detained and deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detention_and_deportation_of_American_citizens_in_the_second_Trump_administration).[^cite_note-Immigration_detention_in_the_second_Trump_administration_Danner_05032025-135] In October 2025, around 3,000 people were detained by ICE in the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area) during [Operation Midway Blitz](81030411.md). Government officials refused or were unable to confirm their whereabouts.[^cite_note-136] In July 2026, 49,571 people were arrested by [United States Immigration and Customs Enforcement](216537.md), the highest monthly arrest total during Trump's second presidential term by that point.[^cite_note-137]&#x20;

## Deportation



During Donald Trump’s first year back in office, the monthly rate of ICE deportation flights more than doubled, and increased to 79 countries compared with 45 during Joe Biden's final year.

[^cite_note-Guardian_20260526-138]

&#x20;Destination countries included many outside the Western hemisphere, some of them to African and Asian countries that had reportedly never received such flights in recent history.

[^cite_note-Guardian_20260526-138]

37,660 people were deported in Trump's first month in office, including both removals and returns, far fewer than the monthly average of 57,000 deportations under Biden in 2024.[^cite_note-ReutersFeb22-139] CBP has reported a drastic decrease in encounters with migrants at the U.S.-Mexico border, suggesting that a decrease in border crossings could explain the decrease in deportations.[^cite_note-140] The Trump administration has claimed that around 140,000 people had been deported as of April 2025, though some estimates put the number at roughly half that number.[^cite_note-Villagran-53] On August 28, 2025, [CNN](62028.md) reported that ICE alone had deported nearly 200,000 people in seven months since Trump returned to office.[^cite_note-auto2-54]&#x20;

An October 27, 2025 DHS press release claimed that more than 2 million people had been removed from the country, with 527,000 being deported while 1.6 million "voluntarily [self-deported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-deportation)".[^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142] The [Center for Migration Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Migration_Studies) called the statement "a self-serving fantasy", describing it as based on a flawed interpretation of population data and that a more realistic number was around 1/10th of claimed self-deportations.[^cite_note-143] The Brookings Institution estimated around 310,000 deported and 210,000 to 405,000 voluntary departures due to enforcement activity.[^cite_note-144]&#x20;

According to a [Marshall Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Project) analysis, "The U.S. government deported more than 21,000 people" —including at least 600 children — "to countries that the State Department deemed too dangerous to visit."[^cite_note-145]&#x20;

### Third-country removals

See also: [D.V.D. v. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.V.D._v._Department_of_Homeland_Security)

Under US immigration law, the government may deport individuals to third countries only when it is "impracticable, inadvisable, or impossible" to return them either to their home countries or send them to any previously designated alternative countries.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147] In 1994, the United States ratified the [United Nations Convention Against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Convention_Against_Torture), prohibiting the expulsion of any person to another country where there are "substantial grounds for believing that he would be in danger of being subjected to torture".[^cite_note-148]&#x20;

In February 2025, the DHS directed immigration officers to review all cases of people granted protections against deportation to their home countries to determine if they could be detained and removed to a third country.[^cite_note-149] Soon after, ICE deported [a Guatemalan man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.V.D._v._Department_of_Homeland_Security#OCG) to Mexico two days after he said in court that he had been abducted and raped in Mexico.[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

According to Robert K. Goldman at [American University Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_University_Law_School), "What the U.S. is doing runs afoul of the bedrock prohibition in U.S. and international law of [non-refoulement](2702190.md)".[^cite_note-151] In a statement, DHS maintained the third-country agreements "ensure due process under the U.S. Constitution" and "are essential to the safety of our homeland and the American people,” while a legal representative of a Cuban detained in Eswatini described a "legal black hole" and a "no man's land" where detainees "have no connection to their new country, and they also apparently have no legal rights.”[^cite_note-shameful-152]&#x20;

On February 13, 2026, the [U.S. Senate Committee on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Senate_Committee_on_Foreign_Relations) issued a Minority Report stating that "the Trump Administration has expanded and institutionalized a system in which the United States urges or coerces countries to accept migrants who are not their citizens, often through arrangements that are costly, wasteful and poorly monitored." The report further found that the United States had "sent more than 32 million dollars to foreign governments in direct connection with third country deportation deals, including those with records of corruption, human rights abuses and human trafficking" without oversight; had made "secret deals with adversarial regimes" such as Iran; and had used third countries to "circumvent U.S. immigration law." According to the report, "Countries With Third Country Deportation Agreements or That Have Received Third Country Nationals" include:[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154]&#x20;

-  [Antigua and Barbuda](951.md)

-  [Belize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belize)

-  [Cabo Verde](18962637.md)

-  [Cameroon](5447.md)

-  [Costa Rica](5551.md)

-  [Dominica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominica)

-  [Ecuador](9334.md)

-  [El Salvador](9356.md)

-  [Equatorial Guinea](9366.md)

-  [Eswatini](27451.md)

-  [Ghana](12067.md)

-  [Guatemala](17238567.md)

-  [Guyana](17238691.md)

-  [Honduras](13394.md)

-  [Kosovo](17391.md)

-  [Liberia](17791.md)

-  [Mexico](3966054.md)

-  [Palau](23372.md)

-  [Panama](22997.md)

-  [Paraguay](23420.md)

-  [Russia](25391.md)

-  [Rwanda](25645.md)

-  [Saint Kitts and Nevis](27198.md)

-  [South Sudan](32350676.md)

-  [Uganda](31816.md)

-  [Uzbekistan](31853.md)

The [Migration Policy Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migration_Policy_Institute) estimated that 15,000 "third-country deportations" of this nature happened in 2025.[^cite_note-155]&#x20;

In late September 2026, [Supreme Court of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberts_Court) allowed the resumption of deportations pending lower court rulings, despite opposition and former rulings.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158]&#x20;

#### Deportation agreements

According to documents obtained by [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) in August 2025, [Honduras](13394.md) and [Uganda](31816.md) signed agreements with the United States to accept deportees from other countries. Honduras agreed to accept up to 200 deportees from other [Spanish-speaking countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanophone) in Latin America, including families with children, while Uganda agreed to accept deportees from other African countries, provided they had no criminal records.[^cite_note-deals-159][^cite_note-160]&#x20;

In April 2026 *[The New York Times](30680.md)* reported [Stephen Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Miller) and other White House aides were pressuring the [U.S. State Department](31975.md) to conclude more deportation agreements with third countries, some of which had been cited by the department for human rights and other violations. Diplomatic cables reviewed by the *Times* said countries that accepted migrants deported from the US could improve their relationships with the US and made suggestions for ways to achieve deportation agreements that could include US payments to foreign security forces, eased visa restrictions, reduced tariffs, US funding of foreign public health programs and reconsideration of a country's placement on US watch lists.[^cite_note-heinous2-161]&#x20;

Citing US diplomatic sources and documents *The New York Times* reported further agreements had been made or were being discussed with [Sierra Leone](27308.md), in exchange for a \$1.5 million payment to the government; the [Central African Republic](5478.md), after the US made an \$85 million donation to an international migration organization helping that country with its refugee issues; with [Burundi](21490998.md) after a pledge of a similar \$8 million donation; and with the [Democratic Republic of Congo](76762.md) (DRC) following a \$50 million donation to the UN High Commission for Refugees (UNHCR).[^cite_note-heinous2-161]&#x20;

#### Africa

According to internal U.S. government documents obtained by [CBS News](359551.md), the Trump administration deported more than 100 undocumented immigrants to eight African countries over a ten day period in August 2026, as part of its broader "third country" deportation campaign. The deportees included nationals from [Afghanistan](737.md), [Cuba](5042481.md), [Nicaragua](21362.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Nepal](171166.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), and [Venezuela](32374.md), and were sent to [Burundi](21490998.md), [Cameroon](5447.md), the [Central African Republic](5478.md), [Equatorial Guinea](9366.md), [Eswatini](27451.md), [Liberia](17791.md), [Rwanda](25645.md), and [Sierra Leone](27308.md). None of the deportees were citizens of the destination countries. The documents indicated that a growing number of African nations—including the Central African Republic, the Democratic Republic of the [Congo](76762.md), and [South Sudan](32350676.md), all of which face armed conflict, political instability, and human rights challenges—had entered into such arrangements with the Trump administration. Some countries, such as [Ghana](12067.md) and Sierra Leone, appeared to have agreed only to accept deportees from other parts of Africa, while others, including [Eswatini](27451.md), the [Central African Republic](5478.md), and [Liberia](17791.md), received detainees from [Latin America](18524.md), [Europe](9239.md), and [Asia](689.md).[^cite_note-cbs2026-162]&#x20;

Among those deported to Africa, some had criminal records in addition to immigration violations; in Eswatini and South Sudan, certain individuals were imprisoned for violent or serious offenses committed in the United States. However, many of the deportees had no criminal history other than entering the U.S. without authorization or lacking proper documentation.[^cite_note-cbs2026-162]&#x20;

The most recent of three ICE flights to Africa discharged deportees in Sierra Leone and the Central African Republic—a landlocked country considered one of the world's most dangerous nations due to protracted armed conflict and the presence of Russian mercenaries, with the U.S. State Department issuing a Level 4 advisory warning [Americans](19792942.md) against all travel there. In response to the CBS News report, the Department of Homeland Security stated that if undocumented immigrants decline to return to their home countries, the government is legally obligated to find an alternative safe country for them, and that otherwise, they are offered a free flight home and a \$3,000 payment. The State Department declined to comment on the leaked documents but reiterated that implementing the Trump administration's immigration policies remained a departmental priority.[^cite_note-cbs2026-162]&#x20;

##### Burundi

[Burundi](21490998.md) announced in late September 2026 that it would accept deportees from the US who had not been accused of terrorism or crimes. Its statement suggested planning was underway for integrating deportees with the local population and appeared to leave open the question of their eventual resettlement in Burundi.[^cite_note-163]&#x20;

##### Cameroon

Reportedly, "documents obtained by The New York Times (...) reveal how the U.S. government used financial pressure and political incentives to secure a deal that the deportees’ lawyer compared to “selling people.”" Diplomats wrote that the US not saying anything about the [2025 Cameroonian presidential election](73432126.md) and the deadly crackdown on the [2025 Cameroonian protests](81442363.md) gave the administration leverage in their negotiations on a deportation agreement. The US also withheld \$30 million in funds for a UN office in [Cameroon](5447.md) until it agreed to the deal. The Cameroonian government "was initially against the agreement and likened it to “blackmail.”[^cite_note-164] Thirty-six deportees have been sent to Cameroon in the first half of 2026.[^cite_note-165]&#x20;

##### Central African Republic

In June 2026, more than two dozen immigrants from countries including [Afghanistan](737.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Turkey](11125639.md), [Armenia](10918072.md), and more, were deported to the [Central African Republic](5478.md).[^cite_note-166] According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), nearly all had court-ordered protections in the US against returning them to their home countries, for fear of persecution.[^cite_note-167]&#x20;

##### Democratic Republic of the Congo

On April 17, 2026, the US deported some 15 refugees from Latin America to the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md) (DRC) under the agreement between the two countries.[^cite_note-168] An attorney in touch with her deported client said the DRC planned to keep the refugees in country for a short time, working with the IOM to arrange "assisted voluntary return" for them to their countries of origin. All those deported were believed to have had legal protection in the US from being sent to those countries. Also in late April, about 1,100 Afghan refugees being held at the [As Sayliyah Army Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As_Sayliyah_Army_Base) in Qatar said they were being forced to choose between returning to [Afghanistan](737.md) or being sent to the DRC. US officials denied any decisions had been made and called their "voluntary resettlement opportunities" a "positive resolution" for their safety. According to UNHCR estimates, the number of displaced people in the DRC could reach 8 million by the end of 2026.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170]&#x20;

##### Equatorial Guinea

On November 10, 2025, US Senator [Jeanne Shaheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeanne_Shaheen) sent a letter to Secretary of State [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) asking for an explanation for a \$7.5 million payment to the government of [Equatorial Guinea](9366.md) in exchange for that nation agreeing to accept US deportees. The letter noted Equatorial Guinea's record of corruption and human rights violations, including several documented in U.S. State Department human rights and human trafficking reports. The payment, made from a fund intended for international relief to migrants and refugees, exceeded total US aid to Equatorial Guinea over the past eight years.[^cite_note-171] It followed approaches and meetings between high US government officials and Equatorial Guinean first vice president [Teodoro Nguema Obiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teodoro_Nguema_Obiang), son and presumed successor of the nation's president. The younger Obiang reportedly received benefits from the US despite his [court settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_settlement) there, conviction in France and subjection to international sanctions for corruption.[^cite_note-172]&#x20;

On May 13, 2026, the UN High Commission for Human Rights issued a statement from experts calling on Equatorial Guinea to "immediately suspend any deportations of individuals at risk, ensure full respect for the principle of [non-refoulement](2702190.md) and guarantee prompt access to legal assistance and effective remedies."[^cite_note-173] It was reported that at least nine individuals who had been deported from the US to Equatorial Guinea had received travel documents and been told they would be returned to their countries of origin despite risks of maltreatment there. According to lawyers for at least 28 deportees in the country, each had received protections from US immigration judges against being returned to their home countries. The director of the Global Strategic Litigation Council called the deportations "the expansion of a deliberate system designed to outsource cruelty and erode protections for people seeking safety in the US."[^cite_note-174] A DHS spokesman claimed the US was "utilizing all lawful options to carry out the largest deportation operation in history" but did not answer detailed questions about US policy or why migrants had not been informed of their destinations before deportation.[^cite_note-175] An [Associated Press](18935732.md) reporter who traveled to the island of [Bioko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioko) with [Pope Leo](54520372.md) in April 2026 interviewed migrants who were being detained in a luxury hotel owned by the Obiang family. The migrants reported substandard conditions and pressures from authorities to return to countries where they may face persecution.[^cite_note-176] A July 2026 report from human rights groups criticized detention conditions in Equatorial Guinea, noting inadequate medical care and access to legal assistance.[^cite_note-inhumane-177] A September 2026 *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)* report noted detainees continued to endure substandard conditions in detention and in addition were being beaten, arrested, moved to prisons notorious for poor conditions and threatened with arrest or death if they did not agree to return to the home countries US authorities were prevented from deporting them to.[^cite_note-178]&#x20;

##### Eswatini

In July 2025, five men were deported to [Eswatini](27451.md),[^cite_note-179] where they were detained in the maximum security correctional complex in [Matsapha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matsapha). Reportedly, all the men, between the ages of 44 and 70, were living in the US after completing sentences for criminal offenses.[^cite_note-heinous-180] Ten more US detainees arrived in early October.[^cite_note-eswatini2-181] The [International Organization for Migration](36893.md) (IOM) also offered assistance to the deportees.[^cite_note-iom-182] The U.S. Department of State's 2023 human rights report on Eswatini noted credible reports of human rights violations including torture of some detainees.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] A Cuban detainee went on a [hunger strike](222292.md) to protest his deportation from the US and confinement in Eswatini; he had lost considerable eyesight from untreated [glaucoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glaucoma).[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-shameful-152] Another of the deportees, a Jamaican man who had been allowed to stay legally in the US since 2021 after serving out a prison sentence for murder, was taken into ICE custody while complying with his reporting requirements. He was transferred between detention facilities in New York, Louisiana and Texas and put on and then taken off a flight to Jamaica. The Jamaican government later stated it would have received him, and he was ultimately returned to Jamaica from Eswatini.[^cite_note-eswatini2-181] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) claimed Eswatini had agreed to accept up to 160 deportees from the US in return for US\$5.1 million,[^cite_note-grifts-186] an amount Eswatini's finance minister later confirmed in testimony before the nation's parliament.[^cite_note-187] In a statement to *The New York Times*, DHS said that immigrants to the US who committed crimes should expect to "end up in [\[Salvadoran detention facility\] CECOT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center), Eswatini, South Sudan, or another third country,"[^cite_note-188] while an attorney for two of those detained claimed "the United States is outsourcing their detention ... to purge the United States of immigrants and refugees and to make a spectacle of deportations."[^cite_note-heinous-180]&#x20;

In August 2025, a coalition of legal and human rights groups asked the High Court of Eswatini to declare the agreement allowing the deportations and detentions unconstitutional.[^cite_note-iom-182][^cite_note-189] The High Court dismissed the case in February 2026, ruling that the groups did not have [legal standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_standing).[^cite_note-190] In March, groups filed a further complaint about the detentions with the [African Commission on Human and Peoples' Rights](1620260.md).[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-Associated_Press-192]&#x20;

In March 2026, Eswatini announced it had received a total of 19 migrants under its agreement with the US, a second individual was about to be repatriated[^cite_note-193] and it was working on repatriating those remaining.[^cite_note-Associated_Press-192] The US deported an additional 11 migrants to Eswatini in July 2026.[^cite_note-194]&#x20;

##### Ghana

On September 5, 2025, the US flew 14 migrants from [Alexandria, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria,_Louisiana) to [Ghana](12067.md), including at least five from [Nigeria](21383.md) and [The Gambia](65656.md) who had immigration court orders preventing their deportation to their home countries.[^cite_note-195] The detainees were reportedly restrained in [straitjackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straitjackets) for the 16-hour flight and not informed of their destination either before or after their arrival. Ghanaian President [John Dramani Mahama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dramani_Mahama) confirmed an agreement with the US to accept deportees on September 10.[^cite_note-196] On September 12, Asian Americans Advancing Justice filed suit on behalf of the five Nigerians and Gambians, four of whom were detained in Dema Camp under substandard conditions. Ghana had sent one former detainee on to The Gambia even though, as a gay man, the US immigration court held he could not be sent there under the provisions of the international Convention Against Torture.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] In late September, eight to ten of these migrants were taken to [Togo](30138.md) where they were left to fend for themselves without identity documents or formal entry. Some of these migrants alleged they had been held in substandard conditions in Ghana and told they would be taken to a hotel before they were expelled.[^cite_note-200]&#x20;

In her decisions on the lawsuit, US District Court Judge [Tanya S. Chutkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanya_S._Chutkan) called the US government's actions "a very suspicious scheme" intended as "an end run around the United States' obligations"[^cite_note-201] but that despite "the government's cavalier acceptance of Plaintiffs' ultimate transfer to countries where they face torture and persecution," her court did not have jurisdiction to rule on the case.[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204]&#x20;

The [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) requested that Ghana refrain from deporting to countries "where there are substantial grounds for believing that they would be in danger of being subjected to torture".[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]&#x20;

In 2025, the US deported more than 30 people to Ghana. Ghana forced at least 22 of those people to return to their home countries, including those who had obtained court-ordered protection in the US to prevent this outcome.[^cite_note-207]&#x20;

##### Liberia

On August 18, 2026, Liberia announced it had agreed to accept up to 1200 US deportees over the next year. As part of the arrangement the US pledged \$124 million in assistance and extended the maximum validity for US visitor visas for Liberians from 12 to 36 months.[^cite_note-208] Liberian government officials said the IOM and UNHCR would provide further assistance to US deportees, who would be free to seek asylum in Liberia. The first flight of 20 deportees including individuals from Venezuela and Cuba arrived August 20.[^cite_note-209] After five of those deported to Liberia refused to deplane, they were told they would be returned to the US but instead were flown to Equatorial Guinea and forced to deplane there where they are being held in substandard conditions.[^cite_note-inhumane-177]&#x20;

##### Libya

On April 18, 2025, US District Judge [Brian E. Murphy](76408780.md) granted a [preliminary injunction](39343.md#Preliminary_injunctions) in [D.V.D. v. Department of Homeland Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.V.D._v._Department_of_Homeland_Security)[^cite_note-Clearinghouse_-_D.V.D._v._Department_of_Homeland_Security-210] requiring the government to follow the law by giving written notice before third-country removals and allowing claims under the UN Convention Against Torture. On May 7, 13 Laotian, Vietnamese, and Philippine immigrants learned that they would be deported the same day to [Libya](17633.md). Murphy prevented the flight with an order clarifying that the flight would violate the injunction.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] [Reuters](18998750.md) found that "at least five men threatened with deportation to Libya in May were sent to their home countries weeks later."[^cite_note-214]&#x20;

##### Rwanda

In mid-August 2025, seven migrants were deported to [Rwanda](25645.md). Rwanda had agreed in June to accept up to 250 migrants from the US, providing them with training, health care and accommodation,[^cite_note-215] in return for US\$7.5 million.[^cite_note-grifts-186] The IOM also offered its assistance to these migrants. The Rwandan government provided no further information about the seven individuals. One activist told [Agence France-Presse](471981.md) that Rwanda had agreed to accept US migrants in return for the money and [an advantage in US-brokered talks](80332368.md) between it and the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](76762.md).[^cite_note-216] These deportations took place despite concerns about Rwanda's human rights record; the U.S. Department of State's most recent human rights report for Rwanda noted numerous concerns including arbitrary killings and arrests, torture and degrading treatment.[^cite_note-217]&#x20;

##### South Sudan

In May 2025, eight men from Cuba, Laos, Mexico, Myanmar, and Vietnam, were told by officials that they were being transferred from an ICE facility in Texas to a facility in Louisiana. However, the plane reportedly flown directly to South Sudan instead flew to [Camp Lemonnier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_Lemonnier) in [Djibouti](17207794.md) after Murphy ordered that the administration "maintain custody".[^cite_note-\:0-218][^cite_note-219] The men were shackled to a shipping container for multiple weeks before being later deported to South Sudan in July 2025.[^cite_note-\:0-218] A US Congressional aide who visited them expressed fears for their safety; [ US Embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Embassy_Juba) officials declined to accompany her on her visit.[^cite_note-shameful-152]&#x20;

##### Uganda

In April 2026, a flight carrying 12 US deportees arrived in Uganda. The [Uganda Law Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uganda_Law_Society) condemned the flight and said it would file challenges to the deportations in national and regional courts.[^cite_note-220]&#x20;

#### Asia

##### Bhutan

Starting in March 2025, the US deported more than 30 refugees, members of the [Llhotshampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lhotshampa_people), a Nepali-speaking minority group, despite not being accepted back by [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan), the nation they had fled to [escape persecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing_of_Lhotshampa_in_Bhutan). [CNN](62028.md) reported that the refugees, who been legally resettled in the United States, were essentially "stateless," living in Nepalese refugee camps.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]&#x20;

#### North America

##### Costa Rica

In the evening of February 20, 2025, the US deported 135 immigrants (including 65 children) from a dozen nations — most predominantly [Uzbekistan](31853.md), [China](5405.md) and [Armenia](10918072.md) — to [Costa Rica](5551.md).[^cite_note-224] Five days later another group of 65 migrants including 16 children arrived.[^cite_note-225] Local authorities immediately loaded them onto buses for a six-hour ride to the [Temporary Migrant Care Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporary_Migrant_Care_Center) near the border with Panama. The center is a former wood products facility that had been extensively remodeled in 2017.[^cite_note-226] The U.S. would reportedly pay for their transportation while the IOM would offer assistance with any onward migration.[^cite_note-227]&#x20;

The office of Costa Rica's independent [ombudsman](26289198.md) (Defender of Inhabitants) met and interviewed the immigrants and issued a report detailing problems with their treatment. The ombudsman's office claimed immigrants had arrived in "visible distress" and were unaware of their destination after leaving the U.S.; were not allowed to contact overseas relatives; had their identity documents taken from them; and did not receive proper medical or other services on arrival. Costa Rica's security minister Mario Zamora stated that the immigrants received proper care on their arrival to the temporary care center and that the ombudsman's report was based on limited contact with immigrants at the airport, a contention the ombudsman rejected.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]&#x20;

As of late April 2025, 85 deportees remained in Costa Rica. They were allowed to leave the care center and permitted to remain in the country for three months, after which they were to have accepted voluntary repatriation or obtained legal status. Without some legal status, however, they were not allowed to obtain work.[^cite_note-231]&#x20;

On June 24, 2025, the [Constitutional Chamber of the Costa Rican Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Constitutional_Chamber_of_the_Costa_Rican_Supreme_Court\&action=edit\&redlink=1) ruled that the 28 migrants, including 13 minors, remaining at the care center be released; given an appropriate legal status in Costa Rica; have their economic and social needs evaluated and receive services to meet them; and be reimbursed for any damages suffered as a result of their confinement, all within 15 days.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233]&#x20;

Earlier, Costa Rican President [Rodrigo Chaves](70007255.md) said his nation was receiving immigrants from the U.S. because it was "helping the economically powerful brother from the north who, if he puts a tax on the free trade zones, will wreck us".[^cite_note-234]&#x20;

On March 23, 2026, Costa Rica signed what it called a "non-binding migration agreement" with the US during a visit from so-called "Shield of the Americas" envoy [Kristi Noem](27663933.md).[^cite_note-235] Costa Rica reportedly agreed to accept up to 25 deportees a week from the US. The deportees would not be from Latin America or countries that would not accept their return. Zamora said they would not have criminal records and would receive assistance "with financial support from the United States" while in Costa Rica. A departing member of the national legislature said the agreement led to "legitimate concerns about the real guarantees of respect for human rights."[^cite_note-236] Between March and July 10, 2026, under this agreement, 12 flights carrying more than 200 people arrived in Costa Rica.[^cite_note-237]&#x20;

##### El Salvador

See also: [March 2025 American deportations of Venezuelans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2025_American_deportations_of_Venezuelans) and [El Salvador–United States relations § Donald Trump's second Presidency](15982838.md#Donald_Trump's_second_Presidency)



United States Secretary of Homeland Security Kristi Noem touring the [Terrorism Confinement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center) in El Salvador on March 26, 2025

On March 14, 2025, the Salvadoran government agreed to detain up to 300 alleged members of [Tren de Aragua](70859925.md) in return for US\$4.76 million. The agreement incorporated several conditions including that none of the funds be used for legal counseling or referrals for the detainees.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] On March 15, [deportation flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2025_Venezuelan_Deportations) operated by [Global Crossing Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Crossing_Airlines)[^cite_note-240] carrying over 260 immigrants from Venezuela and El Salvador arrived in El Salvador where the immigrants were taken into custody at the [Terrorism Confinement Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center) (CECOT). Neither the US nor Salvadoran governments offered any immediate evidence that those deported had been charged with crimes or had connections to any gangs.[^cite_note-241] A [60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes) investigation failed to find any U.S. or foreign criminal charges against 179 of those deported, only finding serious criminal charges against about a dozen.[^cite_note-242] A Cato Institute investigation determined that more than 50 of the Venezuelans deported to El Salvador by the Trump administration had come to the United States legally and broken no immigration laws.[^cite_note-243]&#x20;

US District Judge [James Boasberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Boasberg) ordered that any planes in the air carrying those covered by his order be turned back and those individuals returned to the US. The Trump administration allowed the flights to proceed in spite of his order.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-NYT320-245][^cite_note-246] On April 8, 2025, the Supreme Court ruled that Judge Boasberg was without jurisdiction to issue his order, thus the order was a nullity as [coram non judice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coram_non_judice).[^cite_note-247]&#x20;

On March 28, US District Judge [Brian E. Murphy](76408780.md) issued a [temporary restraining order (TRO)](39343.md#Temporary_restraining_orders) prohibiting the DHS from deporting immigrants to a nation other than that covered in immigration proceedings without a "meaningful opportunity" to make a claim under the [UN Convention against Torture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Convention_against_Torture).[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Despite this order, on March 31, the US sent 17 immigrants it alleged without providing evidence to be members of Tren de Aragua and MS-13 on US military planes to El Salvador to be confined at CECOT.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]&#x20;

In July 2025, 252 of the immigrants incarcerated in CECOT were released in Venezuela - "notably a country that a number of the men had originally fled, fearing persecution."[^cite_note-252] In a March 2026 petition filed with the [Inter-American Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Commission_on_Human_Rights), detainees have detailed abuse and torture, including beatings, sexual assault, being provided unsanitary food and water, and a lack of medical care.[^cite_note-253]&#x20;

##### Panama

[Panama](22997.md) agreed to accept US deportees from countries that US cannot deport directly to. These countries include Afghanistan, China, Iran, Pakistan, and Uzbekistan. Once in Panama, they are governed by Panamanian law and are treated as [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migrant_worker). Several have requested to be repatriated to their countries of origin. Panama is currently housing those who do not want to be repatriated in an immigrant camp. It is unclear what their further processing will involve.[^cite_note-Exports-254]&#x20;

In the evening of February 12, 2025, the first of several U.S. military flights carrying deportees landed in Panama. President Mulino stated some 360 deportees/immigrants would arrive on these flights, be transferred to a camp in [Darién](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darien_Province) and either repatriated or moved to still other countries at U.S. expense. The [International Organization for Migration](36893.md) (IOM) stated it was providing support to these immigrants at Panama's request, as did the [UN High Commissioner for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees).[^cite_note-255]&#x20;

The Panamanian government initially confined these immigrants in the Decapolis Hotel. Attorneys and journalists were largely unable to contact them. Those immigrants able to communicate with journalists said they were asylum seekers who feared persecution or worse if returned to their countries of origin, though U.S. Department of Homeland Security representatives claimed none had asserted such fears during U.S. processing or custody. Legal representatives for the migrants later stated the majority of those flown to Panama had never been given the opportunity to make an asylum claim through the standard "fear interview" required by US law.[^cite_note-fear-256] The immigrants also stated at least one of their number had attempted suicide, while another suffered injuries attempting to escape from confinement.&#x20;

One of the immigrants, Artemis Ghasemzadeh, a 27-year-old English teacher from Iran, said she had [converted from Islam to Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity), which is a capital offense in Iran.[^cite_note-Jervis-257] With a pattern of [persecution of Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians) in Iran, Ghasemzadeh feared that she and other [Iranian Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Iran) would face the death penalty if she were to return there and has not agreed to be repatriated.[^cite_note-Jervis-257][^cite_note-Eisner-258] Ghasemzadeh also said a U.S. soldier who she was helping with translating information for other immigrants asked her not to mention that they were being taken to Panama[^cite_note-Exports-254] and that she had never been asked about or allowed to make a claim for asylum in the U.S.[^cite_note-Fassihi-259] Matthew Soerens, U.S. Director of Church Mobilization for [World Relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Relief), noted that in 2024, "30,000 of the 100,000 refugees resettled in the U.S. were Christians fleeing persecution."[^cite_note-Eisner-258] Peyman Malaz, chief operating officer of the PARS Equality Center, noted that "Those who arrive at the border are often the most persecuted and desperate, such as Iranian Christians".[^cite_note-Jervis-257] Other deported immigrants included a former Afghan military officer, ethnic minorities and LBGTQ+ individuals, and one young adult who had been separated from her family at the U.S. border.[^cite_note-where-260] Ghasemzadeh drew international attention to the group's detention by posting a video that circulated widely and by sending updates to *[The New York Times](30680.md)* using a smuggled mobile phone; a photograph of her writing "Help us" in lipstick on a window of the Panama City hotel became one of the most widely reproduced images of the deportations.[^cite_note-nyt-ghasemzadeh-canada-261] In May 2025, U.S. Representative [Yassamin Ansari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yassamin_Ansari) introduced the Artemis Act, named after Ghasemzadeh, which would bar [expedited removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expedited_removal) of people fleeing countries that the [State Department](31975.md) identifies as persecuting religious minorities and would guarantee them the opportunity to claim asylum before an immigration court; the bill drew support from Democrats but was opposed by the Republican House majority.[^cite_note-artemis-act-262][^cite_note-nyt-ghasemzadeh-canada-261] Ghasemzadeh remained in Panama after most of the group had left, learning Spanish and working as a dental hygienist. In June 2026 the Canadian government granted her asylum, sponsored by a Canadian couple who had read about her case, and she arrived in [British Columbia](3392.md) in early July. Her brother Shahin, who had entered the United States with her and been detained separately, was granted asylum there in 2026.[^cite_note-nyt-ghasemzadeh-canada-261] ["Christian Convert Who Fled Iran and Was Ousted From U.S. Finds a New Home"](https://www.nytimes.com/2026/08/25/world/middleeast/artemis-ghasemzadeh-iran-christian-us-deported-canada-asylum.html). *[The New York Times](30680.md)*. August 25, 2026. Retrieved August 28, 2026.&#x20;

On the night of February 18, Panamanian authorities moved some 170 of the immigrants to the San Vicente camp four hours from [Panama City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_City), which previously housed people detained after crossing the [Darien Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darien_Gap). Journalists and attorneys were still unable to contact the immigrants.[^cite_note-Turkewitz-263] In the meantime, at least one immigrant, reportedly a woman from China, escaped confinement,[^cite_note-264] while another woman from Ireland voluntarily returned there.[^cite_note-265] As of February 28, 2025, there were 9 additional Iranian Christian converts, including three children, detained in the camp. None have agreed to be repatriated to Iran.[^cite_note-Fassihi-259]&#x20;

Panamanian politicians and commentators raised objections to these operations, stating that they were contrary to Panamanian and international law and that Panama should not become "the [Guantanamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp) of Central America".[^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] In mid-March 2025, Panamanian president [José Raúl Mulino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ra%C3%BAl_Mulino) said he did not know if more immigrant flights from the U.S. would arrive in Panama but that he was "not very inclined to do it, because they leave us with the problem".[^cite_note-where-260]&#x20;

In late February 2025, lawyers representing immigrants being held in the Panamanian camp expressed concern that they had not been allowed to communicate with their clients, who they said had been flown out of the U.S. without being screened for asylum. President Mulino told a press conference he did not know why immigrants were being prevented from contacting legal counsel.[^cite_note-268] On March 1, lawyers filed suit against Panama before the [Inter-American Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Commission_on_Human_Rights) (IACHR) charging that the U.S. violated the group's asylum rights, challenging Panama's authority to detain them and asking for emergency orders that none of the detainees be deported to their countries of origin. A separate suit against DHS was anticipated.[^cite_note-269]&#x20;

On March 8, 2025, Panama released the 112 immigrants from San Vicente, gave them permits to remain in the country for 30 days (renewable for an additional 60 days), and bused them to Panama City. On their release, immigrants reported substandard conditions in the San Vicente camp, including limited access to medical treatment; limited communication with families, legal representatives and journalists; and lack of information about their status and possible onward destinations. Several immigrants with possible claims to asylum said those claims had not been considered and that Panamanian authorities had told them "we do not accept asylum." Immigrants' legal representatives expressed concerns that the move was an attempt by Panama to wash its hands of the individuals the US sent to it and to affect the outcome of the case before the IACHR.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] Representatives for the migrants stated that as of mid-May 2025 about half had left Panama, mostly for Mexico, while others remained in shelters provided by UNICEF or local churches.[^cite_note-fear-256] As of late May none of the few applications migrants made for asylum in Panama had been approved while at least nine were rejected, most summarily.[^cite_note-272] Later, the migrants were allowed to remain in Panama until December 2025 but not allowed to work. While some eventually obtained asylum, others continued to face difficulties and they and representatives of charitable organizations complained of governmental indifference and a lack of support.[^cite_note-273]&#x20;

#### South America

##### Paraguay

On April 23, 2026, the US deported 16 individuals from five South American nations and Spain to Paraguay under a previously agreed "safe third country" agreement. According to Paraguay's migration authorities, they were admitted temporarily in order to facilitate their safe return to their countries of origin with the help of the IOM and none had criminal records in their home countries, the US or any other country in which they had resided. Ten more deportees were sent to Paraguay in May, with another 12 in June.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276]&#x20;

### Use of Guantanamo Bay

Further information: [Guantanamo Migrant Operations Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Migrant_Operations_Center)

On January 29, 2025, Trump ordered the preparation of the [Guantanamo Bay detention camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp) to house tens of thousands of immigrants.[^cite_note-277] Since its opening in 2002, Guantanamo Bay previously held 779 detainees, mostly without charges or trial. Detainees had their legal rights restricted and were subjected to [torture](47702.md). The detention camp previously cost half a billion dollars per year, making it the most expensive prison in the world.[^cite_note-278]&#x20;

In February, 178 Venezuelan immigrants were being held in the camp, of whom 126 had criminal records.[^cite_note-\:6-279] On February 20, a Homeland Security official stated "177 of the 178 migrants at Guantánamo Bay were deported...The one other person was sent to a detention facility in the U.S."[^cite_note-280]&#x20;

The Trump administration has faced difficulties using Guantanamo as an immigrant detention facility. These included lack of planning, cost, uncertainty about whether [ICE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICE) or the [U.S. Department of Defense](7279897.md) would be responsible for the immigrants, the lack of policy guidance around support for facilities and services for immigrants, and the questionable legality of the transfers. A bipartisan [congressional delegation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_delegation) was scheduled to travel to the camp, but was cancelled by U.S. Secretary of Defense [Pete Hegseth](34076202.md).[^cite_note-\:4-281]&#x20;

As of March 3, the population of the camp was around 20,[^cite_note-\:4-281] and as of March 13, no immigrants were being held at Guantanamo.[^cite_note-\:5-282] By early April several dozen immigrants "on final orders supposedly to head to their final destination" were being temporarily held at Guantanamo, including Nicaraguans who had been added to a deportation flight originating in Louisiana.[^cite_note-283]&#x20;

In June 2025, after administration efforts to dramatically increase detention of migrants,[^cite_note-284] [Politico](9172097.md) reported on plans to begin sending thousands of those detained to Guantanamo Bay. Those considered for transfer reportedly included 800 Europeans, some from nations considered to be cooperative in receiving their deported citizens.[^cite_note-285] According to U.S. Senator [Gary Peters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Peters), detaining migrants at Guantanamo Bay cost \$100,000 per day per detainee.[^cite_note-286] Days later, those plans appeared to have been put on hold and administration spokespeople denied their existence.[^cite_note-287]&#x20;

### Colombia

On January 26, 2025, Colombian President [Gustavo Petro](13391962.md) barred two U.S. military planes carrying deported Colombian nationals from landing in the country, requesting that deportees be "treated with dignity" and sent to [Colombia](5222.md) on civilian aircraft. Trump and Petro both threatened the other with tariffs. Later that day, U.S. officials assured Colombian officials that they would not place the deportees in handcuffs nor photograph Colombian citizens aboard the flights after they were returned, and that deportees would be escorted by DHS officials instead of military personnel.[^cite_note-288] The White House then announced that Colombia had agreed to allow the planes to land.[^cite_note-289] Former Colombian president [Iván Duque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iv%C3%A1n_Duque) criticized Petro's initial decision and stated on [X](9988187.md): "It is urgent that the Petro government put the country above its populist prejudices and anti-US rhetoric and quickly establish protocols for receiving deported Colombians."[^cite_note-290]&#x20;

### Ecuador

Since 2005, the US has deported thousands of Ecuadorians. In 2023, 18,449 Ecuadorians were deported. In 2024, 13,589 Ecuadorians were deported, according to the Ecuadorian ministry of foreign affairs.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-\:1-292]&#x20;

From January to March 2025, 1,828 Ecuadorians were deported[^cite_note-293] on 18 flights that travel between Texas and [Guayaquil](215586.md) on Tuesdays and Thursdays each week.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-\:1-292]&#x20;

### El Salvador

See also: [Terrorism Confinement Center § Detention of foreign prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center#Detention_of_foreign_prisoners), [MS-13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS-13), and [Deportation of Kilmar Abrego Garcia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Kilmar_Abrego_Garcia)

The second Trump administration continued the Biden administration's deportation of Salvadoran immigrants to their country of nationality. In 2024, an average of ten deportation flights per month arrived in El Salvador from the US.[^cite_note-295] As of early February 2025, a Salvadoran government official stated there had been no massive deportations from the US to El Salvador.[^cite_note-296]&#x20;

[Kilmar Abrego García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Kilmar_Abrego_Garcia), a Salvadoran with [withholding of removal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withholding_of_removal) status in the United States, was mistakenly deported to [El Salvador](9356.md) on March 15, 2025, an action the [Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Donald_Trump) admitted was an "administrative error"[^cite_note-297] while also accusing him of being a member and leader of the [MS-13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MS-13) gang.[^cite_note-\:3-298] Despite a withholding of removal prohibiting his removal to El Salvador due to the risk of persecution, he was detained by ICE and transferred to the Center for Confinement of Terrorism ([CECOT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Confinement_Center)) in Tecoluca.[^cite_note-\:3-298] His wife, a U.S. citizen, and their disabled son filed a lawsuit over the deportation and on April 4 a court ordered the government to return him to the U.S. by April 7.[^cite_note-299] On April 10, the Supreme Court ordered Trump to facilitate his return to the United States, finding his deportation to El Salvador had been improper.[^cite_note-ManganCNBC4.10-62]&#x20;

In a whistleblower complaint filed in June 2024, government attorney Erez Reuveni alleged multiple instances of misconduct around these deportations involving senior Trump administration officials. These included misleading and withholding information from the courts, willfully violating injunctions, keeping information and guidance about injunctions from government agencies, instructing him not to inquire or communicate in writing about possible violations of court orders and making arguments and statements known to be false. Reuveni was fired after refusing to sign an appeal brief in Abrego Garcia's case that included assertions and arguments he believed to be untrue.[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301]&#x20;

### Guatemala

In late January 2025, a Guatemalan governmental official stated that three military flights from the US carrying 265 individuals had arrived in Guatemala.[^cite_note-cbs_24-01-25-302]&#x20;

On August 29, 2025, CNN reported the administration planned to return 600 Guatemalan minors who had arrived in the US unaccompanied.[^cite_note-303] The Guatemalan government stated it had reached a bilateral accord with the US, presented by Department of Homeland Security Secretary [Kristi Noem](27663933.md) during her visit to Guatemala in June, that would guarantee the safe return and family reunification of Guatemalan minors in the US who otherwise might have been placed in custody and provide the returnees with Guatemalan government assistance. The [National Immigration Law Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Immigration_Law_Center) and the Young Center for Immigrant Children's Rights filed a class action suit to block the removals of the Guatemalan minors. After a hearing on August 30, District of Columbia District Judge [Sparkle L. Sooknanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sparkle_L._Sooknanan) ordered that no unaccompanied Guatemalan minors be removed from the US for at least 14 days.[^cite_note-304] Government lawyers confirmed that after the judge's ruling children who had been put on planes headed to Guatemala, at least one of which might have taken off but then returned to the US, were removed and returned to the [Office of Refugee Resettlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Refugee_Resettlement).[^cite_note-305]&#x20;

On September 18, federal district judge [Timothy J. Kelly](54250177.md) extended Judge Sooknanan's emergency order to all Guatemalan minors who entered the US alone and have not exhausted their immigration appeals.[^cite_note-306] Judge Kelly's order enjoins the government from removing these minors from the US. In his order, Judge Kelly, who was appointed by President Trump, noted there was no evidence any of the children's families had asked for their return as the government had claimed; referenced a [whistleblower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whistleblower) report to Congress that at least 30 of the children had been abused in Guatemala;[^cite_note-307] and warned the government against taking his decision as an invitation to remove minors from other countries, some 2,000 of whom are in government facilities.[^cite_note-308]&#x20;

### Iran

In October 2025, a deportation flight of 54 Iranian people landed at [Imam Khomeini International Airport](716542.md) in [Tehran](57654.md).[^cite_note-\:2-309] Two additional deportation flights to Iran were held in December 2025 and January 2026.[^cite_note-\:2-309] A flight was initially planned for March 2026 but was canceled after the United States launched a [war against Iran](82533582.md).[^cite_note-\:2-309]&#x20;

### Mexico

On January 23, 2025, [Mexico](3966054.md) denied a United States military plane the ability to land, causing the plane to never take off while two others bound for Guatemala did. Later that week, White House press secretary [Karoline Leavitt](71739774.md) tweeted that Mexico accepted four deportation flights in one day from the ICE Air Operations and government-chartered flights.[^cite_note-310]&#x20;

On February 14, 2025, Mexican President [Claudia Sheinbaum](57813311.md) said that 13,455 immigrants had been deported to Mexico, 2,970 of whom did not originate from Mexico.[^cite_note-311][^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314] Of the 13,000, 4,300 were Cuban.[^cite_note-315]&#x20;

### Palestine

An investigation by *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* and *[+972 Magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%2B972_Magazine)* identified two deportation flights of Palestinians arrested by ICE utilizing the private plane of [Gil Dezer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gil_Dezer), a business partner and friend of Trump. The flights landed at [Ben Gurion airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Gurion_airport) where Israeli security forces transported the deportees to a [checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_checkpoint) in the [occupied West Bank](59076253.md). Dezer's plane was also used in deportation flights to [Kenya](188171.md), [Liberia](17791.md), [Guinea](12176.md) and [Eswatini](27451.md).[^cite_note-316]&#x20;

### Russia

*The Guardian* reported two deportation flights carrying about 80 Russian asylum applicants left Louisiana for [Cairo](6293.md) in June and August 2025. In Cairo, the deportees were forced onto planes to [Moscow](19004.md). Russian security services interrogated some en route; at least one, a military deserter facing a 10-year sentence in Russia who claimed to have entered the US lawfully, was tied to his seat. Another Russian asylum seeker reported poor conditions and treatment in the [Adams County Correctional Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adams_County_Correctional_Center) in [Natchez, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natchez,_Mississippi) that had caused him to lose 24 kilos. Activists called the deportations "cruel and shameful" and asked Canada to offer asylum to Russian government opponents under US deportation orders. DHS declined to comment on these deportations.[^cite_note-317]&#x20;

### United Kingdom

| \[icon] | **This section is empty.** You can help by [adding to it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Deportation_in_the_second_Trump_administration\&action=edit\&section=). *(September 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

### Flights

The United States cannot unilaterally send deportation flights to other countries; there must be an established agreement with each nation to accept the deportation flights, and they must have diplomatic ties. Some countries, such as China, Cuba, and Venezuela, have largely refused to accept these flights.
